# Indonesian language - Wikipedia

[Jump to content](#bodyContent)

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

Language spoken in Indonesia

This article is about the official language of Indonesia. For an overview on all languages used in Indonesia, see [Languages of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Indonesia "Languages of Indonesia").

**Indonesian**

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/26/Youthpledge.jpg/330px-Youthpledge.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Youthpledge.jpg) The [Youth Pledge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Youth_Pledge "Youth Pledge"), a pledge made by Indonesian youth on October 28, 1928, defining the identity of the Indonesian nation. On the last pledge, there was an affirmation of Indonesian language as a _unifying language_ throughout the archipelago.[Play video](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:WIKITONGUES-_Nila_speaking_Indonesian.webm "Play video")Indonesian language speaker

**Indonesian** (_bahasa Indonesia_) is the [official](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_language "Official language") and [national language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_language "National language") of [Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesia "Indonesia").[\[7\]](#ref-uud) It is a [standardized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_language "Standard language") [variety](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Variety_(linguistics) "Variety (linguistics)") of [Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_language "Malay language"),[\[8\]](#ref-bc) an [Austronesian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austronesian_languages "Austronesian languages") [pluricentric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pluricentric "Pluricentric") [macrolanguage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISO_639_macrolanguage "ISO 639 macrolanguage") that has been used as a [lingua franca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua_franca "Lingua franca") in the multilingual [Indonesian archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_islands_of_Indonesia "List of islands of Indonesia") for centuries. With over 280 million inhabitants,[\[9\]](#ref-9) Indonesia ranks as the [fourth-most populous nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_population "List of countries and dependencies by population") globally. According to the 2020 census, over 97% of Indonesians are fluent in Indonesian,[\[10\]](#ref-Sensus_2020) making it the largest language by number of speakers in [Southeast Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Asia "Southeast Asia") and one of the [most widely spoken languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_languages_by_total_number_of_speakers "List of languages by total number of speakers") in the world.[\[11\]](#ref-The_Indonesian_Language_2004):14 Indonesian vocabulary has been influenced by various native regional languages such as [Javanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javanese_language "Javanese language"), [Sundanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sundanese_language "Sundanese language"), [Minangkabau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minangkabau_language "Minangkabau language"), [Balinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balinese_language "Balinese language"), [Banjarese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banjarese_language "Banjarese language"), and [Buginese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buginese_language "Buginese language"), as well as by foreign languages such as [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic "Arabic"), [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_language "Dutch language"), [Hokkien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hokkien "Hokkien"), [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_language "Portuguese language"), [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit "Sanskrit"), and [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language "English language").

Most Indonesians, aside from speaking the national language, are fluent in at least one of the more than 700 indigenous [local languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Indonesia "Languages of Indonesia"); examples include [Javanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javanese_language "Javanese language") and [Sundanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sundanese_language "Sundanese language"), which are commonly used at home and within the local community.[\[12\]](#ref-policy)[\[13\]](#ref-statistics) However, most formal [education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education "Education") and nearly all national [mass media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_media "Mass media"), [governance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governance "Governance"), [administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_administration "Public administration"), and [judiciary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judiciary "Judiciary") and other forms of communication are conducted in Indonesian.[\[14\]](#ref-hawaii.edu)

Under Indonesian rule from 1976 to 1999, Indonesian was designated as the official language of [East Timor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timor-Leste "Timor-Leste"). It has the status of a [working language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Working_language "Working language") under the country's [constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Timor-Leste "Constitution of Timor-Leste") along with [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language "English language").[\[6\]](#ref-easttimorgovernment.com)[\[15\]](#ref-Berlie2017ch1):3[\[16\]](#ref-thejakartapost.com) In November 2023, the Indonesian language was recognized as one of the official languages of the [UNESCO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNESCO "UNESCO") General Conference.

The term _Indonesian_ is primarily associated with the national standard dialect (_bahasa baku_).[\[17\]](#ref-Sneddon-2003) However, in a looser sense, it also encompasses the various local dialects and varieties spoken throughout the Indonesian [archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archipelago "Archipelago").[\[8\]](#ref-bc)[\[18\]](#ref-18) Standard Indonesian is confined mostly to formal situations, existing in a [diglossic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diglossia "Diglossia") relationship with vernacular Malay varieties, which are commonly used for daily communication, coexisting with the aforementioned regional languages and with [Malay creoles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_trade_and_creole_languages "Malay trade and creole languages");[\[17\]](#ref-Sneddon-2003)[\[12\]](#ref-policy) standard Indonesian is spoken in informal speech as a lingua franca between vernacular Malay dialects, Malay creoles, and regional languages.

## History

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Indonesian_language&action=edit&section=1 "Edit section: History")\]

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8c/Kerinci_MSS_detail.jpg/250px-Kerinci_MSS_detail.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Kerinci_MSS_detail.jpg) [Rencong alphabet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rencong_alphabet "Rencong alphabet"), native writing systems found in central and [South Sumatra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Sumatra "South Sumatra"). The text reads (Voorhoeve's spelling): "haku manangis ma / njaru ka'u ka'u di / saru tijada da / tang \[hitu hadik sa\]", which is translated by Voorhoeve as: "I am weeping, calling you; though called, you do not come" (in modern Malay "Aku menangis, menyeru kau, kau diseru, tiada datang \[itu adik satu\]").[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Prasasti_Kedukan_Bukit_1.jpg/250px-Prasasti_Kedukan_Bukit_1.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Prasasti_Kedukan_Bukit_1.jpg) [Kedukan Bukit Inscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kedukan_Bukit_inscription "Kedukan Bukit inscription"), written in [Pallava script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pallava_script "Pallava script"), is the oldest surviving specimen of the [Old Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Malay_language#Old_Malay_(1st_to_14th_centuries) "History of the Malay language") language.

Standard Indonesian is a [standard language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_language "Standard language") of "Riau Malay",[\[3\]](#ref-Indonesian_Embassy:_Astana)[\[4\]](#ref-Melayu_Online) which despite its common name is not based on the vernacular Malay dialects of the [Riau Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riau_Islands "Riau Islands"), but rather represents a form of [Classical Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Malay_language#Classical_Malay_(14th_to_18th_centuries) "History of the Malay language") as used in the 19th and early 20th centuries in the [Riau-Lingga Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riau-Lingga_Sultanate "Riau-Lingga Sultanate"). Classical Malay had emerged as a literary language in the royal courts along both shores of the [Strait of Malacca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strait_of_Malacca "Strait of Malacca"), including the [Johor Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johor_Sultanate "Johor Sultanate") and [Malacca Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malacca_Sultanate "Malacca Sultanate").[\[19\]](#ref-Abas_1987_26–28)[\[11\]](#ref-The_Indonesian_Language_2004):70[\[20\]](#ref-Nothofer_2009_677–680) Originally spoken in Northeast [Sumatra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumatra "Sumatra"),[\[1\]](#ref-ethnologue) Malay has been used as a [lingua franca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua_franca "Lingua franca") in the Indonesian [archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archipelago "Archipelago") for half a millennium. It might be attributed to its [ancestor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancestor "Ancestor"), the [Old Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Malay_language#Old_Malay_(1st_to_14th_centuries) "History of the Malay language") language (which can be traced back to the 7th century). The [Kedukan Bukit inscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kedukan_Bukit_inscription "Kedukan Bukit inscription") is the oldest surviving specimen of Old Malay, the language used by the [Srivijayan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Srivijaya "Srivijaya") empire.[\[21\]](#ref-1g1H) Since the 7th century, the Old Malay language has been used in [Nusantara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nusantara_(term) "Nusantara (term)") (the Indonesian archipelago), evidenced by Srivijaya inscriptions and by other inscriptions from [coastal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coast "Coast") areas of the archipelago, such as [Sojomerto inscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sojomerto_inscription "Sojomerto inscription").[\[21\]](#ref-1g1H)

Indonesian (in its standard form) has essentially the same material basis as the [Malaysian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Malay "Malaysian Malay") standard of Malay and is therefore considered to be a variety of the pluricentric Malay language. However, it does [differ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comparison_of_Indonesian_and_Standard_Malay "Comparison of Indonesian and Standard Malay") from Malaysian Malay in several respects, with differences in pronunciation and vocabulary, especially within technical and administrative contexts. These differences are due mainly to the Dutch and Javanese influences on Indonesian. Indonesian was also influenced by the _[Melayu pasar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_trade_and_creole_languages "Malay trade and creole languages")_ (lit.'market Malay'), which was the lingua franca of the archipelago in colonial times, and thus indirectly by other spoken languages of the islands.

Malaysian Malay claims to be closer to the classical Malay of earlier centuries,\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\] even though modern Malaysian has been heavily influenced, in lexicon as well as in syntax, by English\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]. The question of whether High Malay (Court Malay) or Low Malay (Bazaar Malay) was the true parent of the Indonesian language is still debated. High Malay was the official language used in the court of the [Johor Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johor_Sultanate "Johor Sultanate") and continued by the Dutch-administered territory of [Riau-Lingga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riau-Lingga_Sultanate "Riau-Lingga Sultanate"), while Low Malay was commonly used in marketplaces and ports of the archipelago. Some linguists have argued that it was the more common Low Malay that formed the base of the Indonesian language.[\[22\]](#ref-22)

### Old Malay as _lingua franca_

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Indonesian_language&action=edit&section=2 "Edit section: Old Malay as lingua franca")\]

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/Srivijaya_Empire.svg/250px-Srivijaya_Empire.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Srivijaya_Empire.svg) For centuries, [Srivijaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Srivijaya "Srivijaya"), through its expansion, economic power and military prowess, was responsible for the wide spread of [Old Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Malay_language#Old_Malay_(1st_to_14th_centuries) "History of the Malay language") throughout the [Malay Archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_Archipelago "Malay Archipelago"). It was the working language of traders and it was used in various ports and marketplaces in the region.[\[23\]](#ref-23)

[Trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade "Trade") contacts carried on by various [ethnic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnicity "Ethnicity") peoples at the time were the main [vehicle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vehicle "Vehicle") for spreading the Old Malay language, which was the main [communications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communication "Communication") medium among the traders. Ultimately, the Old Malay language became a [lingua franca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua_franca "Lingua franca") and was spoken widely by most people in the archipelago.[\[24\]](#ref-24)[\[25\]](#ref-25) The beginning of the common era saw the growing influence of Indian civilisation in the archipelago. With the penetration and proliferation of [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit "Sanskrit") vocabulary and the influence of major [Indian religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_religions "Indian religions") such as [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism "Hinduism") and [Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism "Buddhism"), Ancient Malay evolved into the Old Malay. The oldest uncontroversial specimens of Old Malay are the 7th century CE [Sojomerto inscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sojomerto_inscription "Sojomerto inscription") from [Central Java](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Java "Central Java"), [Kedukan Bukit inscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kedukan_Bukit_inscription "Kedukan Bukit inscription") from [South Sumatra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Sumatra "South Sumatra"), [Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesia "Indonesia") and several other inscriptions dating from the 7th to 10th centuries discovered in [Sumatra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumatra "Sumatra"), [Java](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Java "Java"), other islands of the [Sunda archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunda_Islands "Sunda Islands"), as well as [Luzon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luzon "Luzon"), [Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippines "Philippines"). All these Old Malay inscriptions used either scripts of Indian origin such as [Pallava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pallava_script "Pallava script"), [Nagari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N%C4%81gar%C4%AB_script "Nāgarī script") or the Indian-influenced old Sumatran characters.[\[26\]](#ref-26) The Old Malay system is greatly influenced by [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit "Sanskrit") scriptures in terms of [phonemes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoneme "Phoneme"), [morphemes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morpheme "Morpheme"), [vocabulary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vocabulary "Vocabulary") and the characteristics of scholarship, particularly when the words are closely related to Indian culture. Further research stated that Old Malay and Modern Malay are forms of the same language,[\[27\]](#ref-27)[\[28\]](#ref-28) in spite of some considerable differences between them.

### Classical Malay of Riau-Lingga

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Indonesian_language&action=edit&section=3 "Edit section: Classical Malay of Riau-Lingga")\]

Standard Indonesian is a [standard language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_language "Standard language") which based on "Riau Malay",[\[3\]](#ref-Indonesian_Embassy:_Astana)[\[4\]](#ref-Melayu_Online) although this is a well-known myth,[\[29\]](#ref-:4) as intellectualization of standard language of written and mass-media language is alien to Johor-Riau, Kedah, or Kelantan Malay,[\[29\]](#ref-:4) which despite its common name is not based on the vernacular Malay dialects of the [Riau Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riau_Islands "Riau Islands"), but rather represents a form of [Classical Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Malay_language#Classical_Malay_(14th_to_18th_centuries) "History of the Malay language") as used in the 19th and early 20th centuries in the [Riau-Lingga Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riau-Lingga_Sultanate "Riau-Lingga Sultanate"). Classical Malay had emerged as a literary language in the royal courts along both shores of the [Strait of Malacca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strait_of_Malacca "Strait of Malacca"), including the [Johor Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johor_Sultanate "Johor Sultanate") and [Malacca Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malacca_Sultanate "Malacca Sultanate").[\[19\]](#ref-Abas_1987_26–28)[\[11\]](#ref-The_Indonesian_Language_2004):70[\[20\]](#ref-Nothofer_2009_677–680) The period of Classical Malay started when [Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam "Islam") gained its foothold in the region and the elevation of its status to a [state religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_religion "State religion"). As a result of [Islamisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spread_of_Islam "Spread of Islam") and growth in trade with the [Muslim world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_world "Islamic world"), this era witnessed the penetration of [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic "Arabic") and [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_language "Persian language") vocabulary as well as the integration of major [Islamic cultures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_culture "Islamic culture") with local Malay culture. The earliest instances of Arabic lexicons incorporated in the pre-Classical Malay written in Kawi was found in the [Minye Tujoh inscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minye_Tujoh_inscription "Minye Tujoh inscription") dated 1380 CE from [Aceh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aceh "Aceh") in [Sumatra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumatra "Sumatra"). Nevertheless, pre-Classical Malay took on a more radical form more than half a century earlier as attested in the 1303 CE [Terengganu Inscription Stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terengganu_Inscription_Stone "Terengganu Inscription Stone") as well as the 1468 CE [Pengkalan Kempas Inscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pengkalan_Kempas "Pengkalan Kempas"), both from the Malay Peninsula. Both inscriptions not only serve as the evidence of Islam as a state religion but also as the oldest surviving specimen of the dominant classical [orthographic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthography "Orthography") form, the [Jawi script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jawi_script "Jawi script"). Similar inscriptions containing various adopted Arabic terms with some of them still written the Indianised scripts were also discovered in other parts of [Sumatra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumatra "Sumatra") and [Borneo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borneo "Borneo").[\[30\]](#ref-30)[\[31\]](#ref-Abdul_Rashid_2006_29)

### Competing languages: Dutch-colonial Malay, Bazaar Malay, Javanese, natives languages, and Dutch

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Indonesian_language&action=edit&section=4 "Edit section: Competing languages: Dutch-colonial Malay, Bazaar Malay, Javanese, natives languages, and Dutch")\]

When the [Dutch East India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_East_India_Company "Dutch East India Company") (VOC) first arrived in the archipelago at the start of the 1600s, the Malay language was a significant trading and political language due to the influence of the [Malaccan Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malacca_Sultanate "Malacca Sultanate") and later the [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portugal "Portugal").[\[32\]](#ref-32) However, the language had never been dominant among the population of the Indonesian archipelago as it was limited to mercantile activity. The VOC adopted the Malay language, specifically High Malay, as the administrative language of their trading outpost in the east, but it never gained acceptance neither in Java nor other islands.[\[33\]](#ref-lowc)[\[34\]](#ref-:2) Following the [bankruptcy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bankruptcy "Bankruptcy") of the VOC, the [Batavian Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batavian_Republic "Batavian Republic") took control of the colony in 1799,[\[35\]](#ref-:11) and [Thomas Stamford Raffles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Stamford_Raffles "Thomas Stamford Raffles") during British interregnum promoted Javanese instead of Malay for colonial official by the 1811 language decree and criticized previous policy.[\[34\]](#ref-:2)

> "hitherto the communication with inhabitants of the country has been chiefly through illiterate interpretors \[sic\], or when direct, through the medium of a barbarous dialect of Malays, confounded and confused by the introduction of Portuguese and Dutch"

—Thomas Stamford Raffles

Following the [bankruptcy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bankruptcy "Bankruptcy") of the VOC, the [Batavian Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batavian_Republic "Batavian Republic") took control of the colony in 1799,[\[35\]](#ref-:11) Dutch rule restored,[\[34\]](#ref-:2) and it was only then that education in and promotion of Dutch began in the [colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colony "Colony") because of [Aufklärung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment "Age of Enlightenment"), European liberalism and need for trained officials.[\[33\]](#ref-lowc)[\[36\]](#ref-:8) Based on Regeerings Reglement 1854, the Governor General must took care of education of population which resulted in primary school education happening in Malay and other larger native languages, such as Javanese, Sundanese, and others.[\[36\]](#ref-:8) This created new question of the language of instruction in these educational institutions as Dutch introduction proved too difficult and the local languages and dialects has been so numerous.[\[36\]](#ref-:8) As colonial administration failed to produce Dutch officials with adequate basic Javanese communication ability and Dutch opposition of Dutch usage by educated Javanese, all parties surely forced into Malay even in Java.[\[34\]](#ref-:2)

Hence, the 19th century was the period of strong [Western](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_world "Western world") political and commercial domination in the archipelago. The Dutch colonists, realising the importance of understanding the local languages and cultures, began establishing various centres of linguistic, literary and cultural studies in universities like [Leiden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leiden_University "Leiden University") and [London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_London "University of London"). The use of [Latin script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_script "Latin script") began to expand in the fields of administration and education whereby the influence of [Dutch literatures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch-language_literature "Dutch-language literature") and languages started to penetrate and spread gradually into the language. Nevertheless, it did have a significant influence on the development of Malay in the colony: during the colonial era, the language that would be standardized as Indonesian absorbed a large amount of Dutch vocabulary in the form of [loanwords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loanword "Loanword").

> _"Pertama-tama maka adalah segala tuan-tuan yang menjadi kepala kepada tempat mengajar bahasa Melayu dan lain-lain dalam negeri Singapura yang telah diperbuat oleh tuan Raffles itu, berkehendak mereka itu akan mencari kitab-kitab Bahasa Melayu yang telah termasyur, lagi yang diketahui oleh segala orang Melayu akan dia, supaya boleh diajarkan itu kepada segala kanak-kanak karena yaitu bahasa Melayu betul dan halus adanya, supaya boleh mereka itu berjinak-jinakan dengan bahasa dirinja supaya jangan mereka itu berkacau dan bergaul dengan bahasa kacukan dari pada bahasa bangsa-bangsa yang banyak yang berbahasa dengan bahasa Melayu. Maka jikalau kiranya anak Melayu yang biasa berkampung-kampung dengan bangsa lain-lain seperti Inggeris dan Cina dan Nasrani dan Hindu dan Holanda dan Jawa, maka daripada kecilnya ia telah menengar segala bangsa itu bercampur dirinya dengan bahasa Melayu. Maka didengarnya orang Melayu pun menurutkan perkataan itu. Maka dapat tiada disangka oleh budak itu, inilah bahasa Melayu. Maka nyatalah ini telah kami ketahui. Maka apabila anak Melayu datang dari negeri Jawa, maka kami dengar akan perkataannya itu bercampur dengan perkataan Jawa dan Holanda. Maka sebab hal yang demikian jikalau kanak-kanak belajar bahasa Melayu yang betul seperti Sejarah Melayu, niscaya dapatlah diceraikan oleh mereka itu perkataan dagang dan kasar itu daripada perkataan Melayu betul. Maka sebab perkara yang demikianlah kami hendak ajarkan kepada segala kanak-kanak akan bahasa yang betul supaya bahasa Melayu itu kekal dengan keelokannya dengan tiada berceceran kesana kemari."_ (Malay for "Firstly, the gentlemen who are headmasters of the places where Malay and other languages are taught in Singapore as set up by mister Raffles, they should look for books in Malay that are famous or known to Malays so that they can be taught to children, good and refined Malay that is, so that they become more familiar with their own language so that they will no longer be confused and communicate by way of a language that is a hybrid \[kacukan\] of the languages of the numerous peoples who use the Malay language. If, for instance, a Malay child is used to living together with people of other nations like the English, the Chinese, the Christians, the Hindu, the Dutch, the Javanese, he will hear from his early youth on how all these people mix their own languages with Malay. Then he will listen to Malays on the basis of those wordings, and of course he will then think: this is Malay. Obviously we already know that. When Malays come from Java, we hear that their wording is mixed with Javanese and Dutch words. Because of that if children learn the correct Malay \[Melayu betul\] like the Sejarah Melayu's, they will certainly be able to separate the trade \[dagang\] and rude \[kasar\] words from the correct \[betul\] Malay words. That is why we wish to teach the real language to the children so that Malay will remain in its beauty, without being scattered here and there.")

—Abdullah Abdulkadir's Introduction on his edition of Sejarah Melayu[\[37\]](#ref-:3)

Dutch (and British) tried to freeze 'real' and 'hybrid' Malay by creating a linguistically consistent form of Malay by what they thought as "authoritative" writings ( [_Sejarah Melayu_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_Annals "Malay Annals")) and recognised spoken forms of what they thought as central area of the archipelago, ie. Malacca–Johor Malay used in [Riau–Lingga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riau-Lingga "Riau-Lingga") as "High Malay" which later promoted as "good Malay" and "real Malay" as a medium of communication between the Dutch and local population.[\[37\]](#ref-:3)[\[38\]](#ref-Abdul_Rashid_2006_33) [Governor General Rochussen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jan_Jacob_Rochussen "Jan Jacob Rochussen") after his tour of Java in 1850 suggested for Malay as instructional language as it was the “ [lingua franca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua_franca "Lingua franca")” of the archipelago, while a "special" school for [Inlanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Indonesians "Native Indonesians") should be set up for learning Dutch.[\[36\]](#ref-:8) There was possibility that the colonial authorities inspired by [Abdullah Abdulkadir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdullah_Abdul_Kadir "Abdullah Abdul Kadir"), a famous language teacher and clerk (1797–1854), who define the real Malay (Melayu sungguh) by Sejarah Melayu as example of the most elegant form of Malay and heterogenization of Malay \[by bastard–trade and rude words\] had to be stopped.[\[37\]](#ref-:3) The language was also taught in schools not only in Riau but also in [East Sumatra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_East_Sumatra "State of East Sumatra"), [Java](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Java "Java"), [Kalimantan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalimantan "Kalimantan") and East Indonesia.[\[38\]](#ref-Abdul_Rashid_2006_33) In Riau–Johor area, writing was highly respected due to [Raja Ali Haji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raja_Ali_Haji "Raja Ali Haji") and his relatives whom perhaps did not think that Abdullah to be worth to mention.[\[37\]](#ref-:3) Most European colonial scholars (and authorities) assumed that preference should be given to manuscript over spoken form in the area and in other area, thus they dismissed writings from [Sino-Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_trade_and_creole_languages#Peranakan-Baba_Malay "Malay trade and creole languages") press, dismissed writings from [Kedah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kedah "Kedah"), [Kelantan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kelantan "Kelantan"), [Ambon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambon,_Maluku "Ambon, Maluku"), [Java](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Java "Java"), and [Menado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menado "Menado"), and laughed the so-called "gibberish Malay" ( [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_language "Dutch language"): _brabbel-Maleisch_) spoken form.[\[37\]](#ref-:3)[\[34\]](#ref-:2) In 1864, the Dutch colonial government therefore decided to disseminate this language ―and not Dutch― throughout the colony.[\[39\]](#ref-:1)[\[40\]](#ref-40) To this end, the colonial government stimulated the study, standardisation and modernisation of Malay, imposing it via its institutions, via education, the missions and the media, and via the literary works produced by the state publishers [Balai Poestaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balai_Pustaka "Balai Pustaka"). In this respect, the Dutch pursued a non-chauvinistic cultural policy.[\[39\]](#ref-:1) Another catalyst in the movement towards standardization of Malay in Western script was an amalgam of philology and a growing consciousness of an Indies identity such that a " _lingua franca_" justification for Malay had become insufficient.[\[34\]](#ref-:2) This 'real' and 'good' Malay challenged and resisted by daily life, in both spoken and written, Malay.[\[37\]](#ref-:3) In reality, Klinkert's pure Malacca or Riau Malay was unusable in the eastern part of Indies even in the coastal regions.[\[34\]](#ref-:2) In 1910, Sasrasoeganda Koewatin, a Javanese of prominent Malay language teacher of Kweekschool and OpIeidingschool in [Yogyakarta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yogyakarta "Yogyakarta"), wrote a Malay grammar book entitled _Kitab Jang Menjatakan Djalannja Bahasa Melajoe_ in which is the first Malay grammar book in Latin script which became the basis for the Indonesian language in use today.[\[41\]](#ref-41)

> Any one with the courage to look calmly into the future and yet with concern enough for his children and grand-children and enough feeling for reality and justice not to dream of eternal domination, should not expect a permanent relationship between them other country and the colony, but rather make every effort to ensure that some portion of what we have gained and created with so much toil and trouble be preserved for as long as possible. There is no more appropriate means to this end than the dissemination of our language. As with every communication of intellectual substance we shall have gained personal satisfaction for ourselves, and have laid the ground-work for an abiding unity of interests…. If we want to promote Indonesian unity, let us begin first with the highest social classes, with the elite: and then, as the British did in India and the French in Annam, we must institute a language which can represent international culture fully as the general medium for social intercourse. In Indonesia this language will have to be Dutch….

—Nieuwenhuis, G. J. (1952), Het Nederlandsch in Indie. Groningen, J. B. Wolters.[\[36\]](#ref-:8)

The rapid disappearance of Dutch was a very unusual case compared with other colonized countries, where the colonial language generally has continued to function as the language of politics, [bureaucracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bureaucracy "Bureaucracy"), education, [technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Technology "Technology"), and other fields of importance for a significant time after independence.[\[42\]](#ref-Swaan) The Indonesian scholar [Soenjono Dardjowidjojo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soenjono_Dardjowidjojo?action=edit&redlink=1 "Soenjono Dardjowidjojo (page does not exist)")\[ [id](https://id.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soenjono%20Dardjowidjojo "id:Soenjono Dardjowidjojo")\] even goes so far as to say that when compared to the situation in other Asian countries such as [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India "India"), [Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysia "Malaysia"), [Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore "Singapore") and [the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippines "Philippines"), "Indonesian is perhaps the only language that has achieved the status of a national language in its true sense" since it truly dominates in all spheres of [Indonesian society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Indonesia "Culture of Indonesia").[\[43\]](#ref-43) The ease with which Indonesia eliminated the language of its former colonial power can perhaps be explained as much by Dutch [policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Policy "Policy") as by [Indonesian nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_nationalism "Indonesian nationalism"). In marked contrast to the [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/France "France"), [Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spain "Spain") and Portuguese, who pursued an [assimilation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_assimilation "Cultural assimilation") colonial policy, or even the [British](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire "British Empire"), the Dutch did not attempt to spread their language among the indigenous population.[\[42\]](#ref-Swaan) At the beginning, when [Mr. J. H. Abendanon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Henrij_Abendanon?action=edit&redlink=1 "Jacques Henrij Abendanon (page does not exist)")\[ [nl](https://nl.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques%20Henrij%20Abendanon "nl:Jacques Henrij Abendanon"); [id](https://id.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques%20Henrij%20dan%20Rosa%20Abendanon "id:Jacques Henrij dan Rosa Abendanon")\] held the Director of the Department of Education in 1900, he ran efforts such as institution of Dutch courses in people’s schools and later institution of Dutch as compulsory subjects in those schools from third to sixth grades to spread the use of Dutch as he was convinced that Dutch knowledge would be the shortest way to receive Western culture.[\[36\]](#ref-:8) Meanwhile, Dr. G. J. Nieuwenhuis thought that Dutch as a unifying force in the Indies as an instrument of both cultural and economic expansion.[\[36\]](#ref-:8) In 1908, Budi Utomo, a congress of [Inlanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Indonesians "Native Indonesians"), asked to relax the admission standards of Dutch schools which reflected unsatisfaction of [Inlanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Indonesians "Native Indonesians") because of limited Dutch-education opportunity for limited Dutch language ability as Dutch language ability led to important position for high paying job and belonging of new upper class in the society.[\[36\]](#ref-:8) Dr. J. W. Meyer Ranneft opposed Western education for Indonesian for unknown economic or cultural consequences.[\[36\]](#ref-:8) Even then, Dutch administrators were remarkably reluctant to promote the use of Dutch compared to other colonial regimes. For example, Dutch officials communicated in Officialese Malay ( [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_language "Dutch language"): _dienst-Maleisch_, [lit.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literal_translation "Literal translation")'Service Malay'), 'Gibberish Malay' or Dutch to Inlanders counterpart which the Inlanders counterpart expected to reply in High Javanese or Malay \[but not in Dutch\].[\[34\]](#ref-:2) Dutch thus remained the language of a small elite: in 1940, only 2% of the total population could speak Dutch. In fact, they consciously prevented the language from being spread by refusing to provide education, especially in Dutch, to the [Inlanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Indonesians "Native Indonesians") so they would not come to see themselves as equals, as steadily increasing Inlanders entered secondary and higher education, went occupying important government positions and kept competing for more privileges.[\[36\]](#ref-:8)[\[34\]](#ref-:2)[\[42\]](#ref-Swaan) Moreover, the Dutch wished to prevent the Indonesians from elevating their perceived social status by taking on elements of Dutch culture. Thus, until the 1930s, they maintained a minimalist regime and allowed Malay to spread quickly throughout the archipelago. The [nationalist movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_National_Awakening "Indonesian National Awakening") that ultimately brought Indonesian to its national language status rejected [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_language "Dutch language") from the outset.[\[42\]](#ref-Swaan) Nevertheless, the usage of Dutch words in Malay became a symbol of high social status which remain until years after independence despite heavy critic against it.[\[44\]](#ref-:5)

### The birth of Indonesian: adoption as the national language

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Indonesian_language&action=edit&section=5 "Edit section: The birth of Indonesian: adoption as the national language")\]

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/MuseumSumpahPemuda-13-IndonesischClubgebouw.jpg/250px-MuseumSumpahPemuda-13-IndonesischClubgebouw.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:MuseumSumpahPemuda-13-IndonesischClubgebouw.jpg) The [Youth Pledge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Youth_Pledge "Youth Pledge") was the result of the Second Youth Congress held in [Batavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batavia,_Dutch_East_Indies "Batavia, Dutch East Indies") in October 1928. On the last pledge, there was an affirmation of Indonesian language as the _unifying language_ throughout the archipelago.[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f6/COLLECTIE_TROPENMUSEUM_Opening_van_de_Volksraad_door_gouverneur-generaal_Van_Limburg_Stirum_op_18_mei_1918_op_Java_TMnr_10001373.jpg/250px-COLLECTIE_TROPENMUSEUM_Opening_van_de_Volksraad_door_gouverneur-generaal_Van_Limburg_Stirum_op_18_mei_1918_op_Java_TMnr_10001373.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:COLLECTIE_TROPENMUSEUM_Opening_van_de_Volksraad_door_gouverneur-generaal_Van_Limburg_Stirum_op_18_mei_1918_op_Java_TMnr_10001373.jpg) _[Volksraad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volksraad_(Dutch_East_Indies) "Volksraad (Dutch East Indies)")_ session held in July 1938 in Jakarta, where Indonesian was formally used for the first time by Jahja Datoek Kajo

The [nationalist movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_National_Awakening "Indonesian National Awakening") that ultimately brought Indonesian to its national language status rejected [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_language "Dutch language") from the outset. Despite Dutch dominance at that time covered nearly all aspects, with official forums requiring the use of Dutch, although since the Second Youth Congress (1928) the use of Indonesian as the national language was agreed on as one of the tools in the independence struggle. The term _bahasa Indonesia_ (Indonesian) itself had been proposed by [Mohammad Tabrani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Tabrani "Mohammad Tabrani") in 1926,[\[45\]](#ref-45) and Tabrani had further proposed the term over calling the language [Malay language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_language "Malay language") during the First Youth Congress in 1926.[\[2\]](#ref-Cambridge_University_Press) Hence, in the congress, Indonesian replaced Malay as descriptor of the language which settled the nomenclature and its place in society.[\[36\]](#ref-:8) Several years prior to the congress, Swiss linguist, [Renward Brandstetter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renward_Brandstetter "Renward Brandstetter") wrote _[An Introduction to Indonesian Linguistics](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/An%20Introduction%20to%20Indonesian%20Linguistics "s:An Introduction to Indonesian Linguistics")_ in 4 essays from 1910 to 1915. The essays were translated into English in 1916. By "Indonesia", he meant [the name of the geographical region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Names_of_Indonesia#Indonesia "Names of Indonesia"), and by "Indonesian languages" he meant [Malayo-Polynesian languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malayo-Polynesian_languages "Malayo-Polynesian languages") west of New Guinea, because by that time there was still no notion of Indonesian language. As of it, [Mohammad Hoesni Thamrin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Husni_Thamrin "Mohammad Husni Thamrin") inveighed actions underestimating Indonesian. After some criticism and protests, the use of Indonesian was allowed since the _[Volksraad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volksraad_(Dutch_East_Indies) "Volksraad (Dutch East Indies)")_ sessions held in July 1938.[\[46\]](#ref-46) Dutch realised the need to counter the spread of Malay by teaching Dutch to the natives, but it was too late.[\[47\]](#ref-:0) In 1942, the Japanese conquered Indonesia. The Japanese mandated that all official business be conducted in Indonesian and quickly outlawed the use of the Dutch language.[\[47\]](#ref-:0) Three years later, the Indonesians themselves formally abolished the language and established _bahasa Indonesia_ as the national language of the new nation.[\[48\]](#ref-Paauw)

> _Jang dinamakan "bahasa Indonesia" jaitoe bahasa Melajoe jang soenggoehpoen pokoknja berasal dari "Melajoe Riau" akan tetapi jang soedah ditambah, dioebah atau dikoerangi menoeroet keperloean zaman dan alam baharoe, hingga bahasa itoe laloe moedah dipakai oleh rakjat di seloeroeh Indonesia; pembaharoean bahasa Melajoe hingga kini mendjadi bahasa Indonesia itoe haroes dilakoekan oleh kaoem ahli jang beralam baharoe, ialah alam kebangsaan Indonesia._ (Indonesian for "What is called "Indonesian" is the Malay which originally came from "Riau Malay" but which has been added to, changed or modified according to the needs of the times and the new world, so that the language is now used by the people throughout Indonesia; the renewal of the Malay until now to become Indonesian must be carried out by experts who are from the new world, namely the Indonesian national world.")

—Excerpt of [Ki Hadjar Dewantara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ki_Hajar_Dewantara "Ki Hajar Dewantara")'s "Bahasa Indonesia didalam pergoroean", a paper presented at the First Indonesian Language Conference at [Surakarta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surakarta "Surakarta"), 25–28 June 1938.[\[49\]](#ref-KBI1&2@Lampau)[\[50\]](#ref-50)[\[51\]](#ref-51)

> _"Bahasa Indonesia ialah bahasa Melajoe Perhoeboengan, jang diperkaja dengan zat-zat dari Melajoe Kesoesastraan, bahasa Djawa, bahasa Belanda dan dengan lebih koerang bahasa Austronesia jang Iain-lain, sedang peroebahan saraf banjak terdjadi dan begitoe poela peroebahan tinggi boenji dan tekanan. Bahasa Indonesia soedah djadi bahasa keboedajaan dan akan toemboeh dengan keboedajaan Indonesia."_ (Indonesian for "Indonesian is a communication Malay, enriched with elements from literary Malay, Javanese, Dutch and to a greater extent other Austronesian languages, while many grammar changes have occurred and so have changes in the pitch and stress. Indonesian has become the language of culture and will grow with Indonesian culture.")

—Excerpt of [Sanoesi Pane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanusi_Pane "Sanusi Pane")'s "Sedjarah Bahasa Indonesia", a paper presented at the First Indonesian Language Conference at [Surakarta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surakarta "Surakarta"), 25–28 June 1938.[\[52\]](#ref-52)

The pledge on national language implied the obligation on developing Indonesian, to replace the Dutch, as a mean of entry into modern world culture.[\[36\]](#ref-:8) _Kacukan_ (literally "hybrid, mixed," means "non- [Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_Indonesians "Malay Indonesians")") Indonesians, for examples politicians such as [Hatta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Hatta "Mohammad Hatta"), [Njoto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Njoto "Njoto"), and [Soekarno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soekarno "Soekarno"), journalists such as [B.M. Diah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B.M._Diah "B.M. Diah") and [Mochtar Lubis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mochtar_Lubis "Mochtar Lubis"), writers such as [Armijn Pane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armijn_Pane "Armijn Pane"), [Takdir Alisjahbana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sutan_Takdir_Alisjahbana "Sutan Takdir Alisjahbana"), [Hamka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamka "Hamka"), [Sitor Situmorang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sitor_Situmorang "Sitor Situmorang"), and [Pramoedya Ananta Toer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pramoedya_Ananta_Toer "Pramoedya Ananta Toer"), were playing predominant role in the standardization of Indonesian.[\[37\]](#ref-:3) [Sutan Takdir Alisjahbana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sutan_Takdir_Alisjahbana "Sutan Takdir Alisjahbana") was a great promoter of the use and development of Indonesian and he was greatly exaggerating the decline of Dutch. Higher education was still in Dutch and many educated Indonesians were writing and speaking in Dutch in many situations (and were still doing so well after independence was achieved). He believed passionately in the need to develop Indonesian so that it could take its place as a fully adequate national language, able to replace Dutch as a means of entry into modern international culture. In 1933, he began the magazine _[Pujangga Baru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poedjangga_Baroe "Poedjangga Baroe")_ (New Writer — _Poedjangga Baroe_ in the original spelling) with co-editors [Amir Hamzah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amir_Hamzah "Amir Hamzah") and [Armijn Pane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armijn_Pane "Armijn Pane"). The language of Pujangga Baru came in for criticism from those associated with the more classical School Malay and it was accused of publishing Dutch written with an Indonesian vocabulary. Alisjahbana would no doubt have taken the criticism as a demonstration of his success. To him the language of Pujangga Baru pointed the way to the future, to an elaborated, Westernised language able to express all the concepts of the modern world. As an example, among the many innovations they condemned was use of the word _bisa_ instead of _dapat_ for 'can'. In Malay _bisa_ meant only 'poison from an animal's bite' and the increasing use of Javanese _bisa_ in the new meaning they regarded as one of the many threats to the language's purity. Unlike more traditional intellectuals, he did not look to Classical Malay and the past. For him, Indonesian was a new concept; a new beginning was needed and he looked to Western civilisation, with its dynamic society of individuals freed from traditional fetters, as his inspiration.[\[11\]](#ref-The_Indonesian_Language_2004) The same group took the initiative to gather on the First Indonesian Language Congress in Surakarta in 1938 which passed resolution on need of language institute and faculty for Indonesian, decision on terms, creation of new orthography, codification of new grammar, and agreement on Indonesian as language of law and language in various bodies. Nevertheless, the congress resolution remained on paper as there was no power to put them on effect, which came later during the Japanese occupation.[\[36\]](#ref-:8) By borrowing heavily from numerous other languages, it expresses a natural linguistic evolution and so is as natural as the next language, as demonstrated in its capacity to absorb foreign vocabulary. In syntaxis, Indonesian departs from the concrete nature of the Malay sentence into European abstractions.[\[53\]](#ref-:6) Hence, Indonesian was a creation, a hybrid of various form of Malay, mainly regulated by European linguistics from [van Ophuijsen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Adriaan_van_Ophuijsen?action=edit&redlink=1 "Charles Adriaan van Ophuijsen (page does not exist)")\[ [nl](https://nl.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles%20Adriaan%20van%20Ophuijsen "nl:Charles Adriaan van Ophuijsen"); [id](https://id.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles%20Adriaan%20van%20Ophuijsen "id:Charles Adriaan van Ophuijsen"); [ms](https://ms.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles%20Adriaan%20van%20Ophuijsen "ms:Charles Adriaan van Ophuijsen")\] (through grammar book “ _Spraakkunst van het Maleisch_” (1910) and wordbook “ _Kitab Logat Melayu_” (1896)) and [Spat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claas_Spat?action=edit&redlink=1 "Claas Spat (page does not exist)") instead of [Raja Ali Haji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raja_Ali_Haji "Raja Ali Haji") — Malayan Peninsular Malay (or [Malaysian Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Malay "Malaysian Malay")) mainly based on [Zainal Abidin bin Ahmad (Za'ba)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zainal_Abidin_Ahmad_(writer) "Zainal Abidin Ahmad (writer)")’s “ _Ilmu Mengarang Melayu_” and the “ _Pelita Bahasa Melayu_” as the R. O. Winstedt’s Malay grammar (1914) was not influential.[\[37\]](#ref-:3)[\[54\]](#ref-54)

| English | Indonesian | Standard Malay |
| --- | --- | --- |
| the gathering consisted of great people | perkumpulan itu terdiri dari orang besar | perhimpunan itu terdiri daripada orang-orang hebat |
| It depends on people's stance on the question of which language to use | bermatjam2-lah pendirian orang terhadap kapada soal bahasa mana yang harus di-pakai | Ia bergantung kepada pendirian orang ramai terhadap persoalan bahasa mana yang hendak digunakan |
| there is a big crowd | ada keramaian besar | ada orang ramai |

Indonesian change from concrete nature of Malay into European abstraction[\[53\]](#ref-:6)

### Indonesian language in Japanese occupation, Old Order, and New Order

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Indonesian_language&action=edit&section=6 "Edit section: Indonesian language in Japanese occupation, Old Order, and New Order")\]

Once the [Japanese overturned Dutch rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_occupation_of_the_Dutch_East_Indies "Japanese occupation of the Dutch East Indies"), a prohibition on the use of the Dutch language led to an expansion of Indonesian language newspapers and pressure on them to increase the language's wordstock. The Japanese agreed to the establishment of the [Komisi Bahasa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Komisi_Bahasa?action=edit&redlink=1 "Komisi Bahasa (page does not exist)") (Language Commission) in 20 October 1942,[\[55\]](#ref-Sanyo_Kaigi's_Answer) formally headed by three Japanese but with a number of prominent Indonesian intellectuals playing the major part in its activities. Soewandi, later to be Minister of Education and Culture, was appointed secretary, Alisjahbana was appointed an 'expert secretary' and other members included the future president and vice-president, Sukarno and Hatta.[\[11\]](#ref-The_Indonesian_Language_2004) Journalists, beginning a practice that has continued to the present, did not wait for the _Komisi Bahasa_ to provide new words, but actively participated themselves in coining terms. Many of the Komisi Bahasa's terms never found public acceptance and after the Japanese period were replaced by the original Dutch forms, including _jantera_ (Sanskrit for 'wheel'), which temporarily replaced _mesin_ (machine), _ketua negara_ (literally 'chairman of state'), which had replaced _presiden_ (president) and _kilang_ (meaning 'mill'), which had replaced _pabrik_ (factory). In a few cases, however, coinings permanently replaced earlier Dutch terms, including _pajak_ (earlier meaning 'monopoly') instead of _belasting_ (meaning 'tax') and _senam_ (meaning 'exercise') instead of _gimnastik_ (gymnastics). The Komisi Bahasa is said to have coined more than 7000 terms,[\[36\]](#ref-:8) although few of these gained common acceptance.[\[11\]](#ref-The_Indonesian_Language_2004)

> _…soepaja nama Bahasa Indonesia ditetapkan dengan resmi, soepaja djangan menimboelkan kekeliroean djoega dengan bahasa Melajoe. Nama “Bahasa Melajoe” jang selaloe membangoenkan perasaän provincialisme, dan karena itoe menahan timboelnja rasa persatoean jang koeat jang begitoe perloe oentoek menjelengga-rakan kemenenangan achir, hendaklah dihapoeskan sebagai nama bahasa oemoem disini dan diganti dengan “Bahasa Indonesia”._ (Indonesian for "…so that the name Bahasa Indonesia is officially established, so that it does not cause confusion with the Malay language. The name of “Malay language” which always arouses feelings of provincialism, and because it suppresses the emergence of a strong sense of unity which must therefore bring about final victory, should be rejected as the name of the general language here and replaced with “Bahasa Indonesia”")

—Excerpt of "Sanyo Kaigi's Answer to the Question: "How to concludes the Indonesian Language?""[\[55\]](#ref-Sanyo_Kaigi's_Answer)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/Poster_Propaganda_Jepang.jpg/250px-Poster_Propaganda_Jepang.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Poster_Propaganda_Jepang.jpg) [Propaganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Propaganda "Propaganda") posters from [Japanese-occupied Dutch East Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_occupation_of_the_Dutch_East_Indies "Japanese occupation of the Dutch East Indies")

The adoption of Indonesian as the country's national language was in contrast to most other post-colonial states. Neither the language with the most native speakers (Javanese) nor the language of the former European colonial power (Dutch) was to be adopted. Instead, a local language with far fewer native speakers than the most widely spoken local language was chosen (nevertheless, Malay was the second most widely spoken language in the colony after Javanese, and had many L2 speakers using it for trade, administration, and education).

In 1945, when [Indonesia declared its independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proclamation_of_Indonesian_Independence "Proclamation of Indonesian Independence"), Indonesian was formally declared the national language,[\[7\]](#ref-uud) despite being the native language of only about 5% of the population. In contrast, Javanese and Sundanese were the mother tongues of 42–48% and 15% respectively.[\[56\]](#ref-Kratz) The combination of [nationalistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalism "Nationalism"), [political](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics "Politics"), and [practical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pragmatism "Pragmatism") concerns ultimately led to the successful adoption of Indonesian as a national language. In 1945, Javanese was easily the most prominent language in Indonesia. It was the native language of nearly half the population, the primary language of politics and [economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economics "Economics"), and the language of [courtly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_court "Royal court"), [religious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion "Religion"), and literary tradition.[\[42\]](#ref-Swaan) What it lacked, however, was the ability to unite the diverse Indonesian population as a whole. With thousands of islands and hundreds of different languages, the newly independent country of Indonesia had to find a national language that could realistically be spoken by the majority of the population and that would not divide the nation by favouring one ethnic group, namely the Javanese, over the others. In 1945, Indonesian was already in widespread use;[\[56\]](#ref-Kratz) in fact, it had been for roughly a thousand years. Over that long period, Malay, which would later become standardized as Indonesian, was the primary language of [commerce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commerce "Commerce") and [travel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Travel "Travel"). It was also the language used for the propagation of [Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam "Islam") in the 13th to 17th centuries, as well as the language of instruction used by Portuguese and Dutch missionaries attempting to convert the indigenous people to [Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity "Christianity").[\[42\]](#ref-Swaan) The combination of these factors meant that the language was already known to some degree by most of the population, and it could be more easily adopted as the national language than perhaps any other. Moreover, it was the language of the sultanate of [Brunei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brunei "Brunei") and of future [Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysia "Malaysia"), on which [some Indonesian nationalists had claims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Indonesia "Greater Indonesia").

Over the first 53 years of [Indonesian independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proclamation_of_Indonesian_Independence "Proclamation of Indonesian Independence"), the country's first two presidents, [Sukarno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukarno "Sukarno") and [Suharto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suharto "Suharto"), constantly nurtured the sense of national unity embodied by Indonesian, and the language remains an essential component of Indonesian identity. Through a language planning program that made Indonesian the language of [politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics "Politics"), [education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education "Education"), and nation-building in general, Indonesian became one of the few success stories of an [indigenous language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_language "Indigenous language") effectively overtaking that of a country's colonisers to become the _[de jure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De_jure "De jure")_ and _[de facto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De_facto "De facto")_ official language.[\[48\]](#ref-Paauw) On 18 June 1947, the new Language Commission (The Working Committee on the Indonesian Language) under S. Takdir Alisjahbana was established and gained another 5000 new terms which later became a part of the Linguistic and Cultural Institute of the Faculty of Literature and Philosophy in the University of Indonesia, under Dr. Prijana and coined new tems which were published in journals such as “Medan Bahasa” and “Bahasa dan Budaja” and later in dictionaries. Meanwhile, W. J. S. Poerwadarminta made an excellent dictionary for Indonesian in 1953.[\[36\]](#ref-:8) In the 1970–80s under Suharto, the [Center for Language Development and Construction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agency_for_Language_Development_and_Cultivation "Agency for Language Development and Cultivation") launched several campaigns to propagate " _bahasa Indonesia yang baik dan benar_" (good and correct Indonesian).[\[37\]](#ref-:3) Today, Indonesian continues to function as the language of [national identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_identity "National identity") as the Congress of Indonesian Youth envisioned, and also serves as the language of education, [literacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literacy "Literacy"), [modernization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernization_theory "Modernization theory"), and [social mobility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_mobility "Social mobility").[\[48\]](#ref-Paauw) Despite still being a second language to most Indonesians, it is unquestionably the language of the Indonesian nation as a whole, as it has had unrivalled success as a factor in nation-building and the strengthening of Indonesian identity.

### Modern standard and colloquial contemporary Indonesian

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Indonesian_language&action=edit&section=7 "Edit section: Modern standard and colloquial contemporary Indonesian")\]

Further information: [Indonesian slang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_slang "Indonesian slang")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b8/Sukarno_hatta_airport_-_Terminal_-_Jakarta_-_Indonesia.jpg/250px-Sukarno_hatta_airport_-_Terminal_-_Jakarta_-_Indonesia.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Sukarno_hatta_airport_-_Terminal_-_Jakarta_-_Indonesia.jpg) Road signs in an [airport terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airport_terminal "Airport terminal")[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7f/Kuta_Bali_Indonesia_Tol-Station-Nusa-Dua-01.jpg/250px-Kuta_Bali_Indonesia_Tol-Station-Nusa-Dua-01.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Kuta_Bali_Indonesia_Tol-Station-Nusa-Dua-01.jpg) Toll gate in [Bali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bali "Bali")[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/Jakarta_Indonesia_Bus-stop-Monumen-Nasional-02.jpg/250px-Jakarta_Indonesia_Bus-stop-Monumen-Nasional-02.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Jakarta_Indonesia_Bus-stop-Monumen-Nasional-02.jpg) Indonesian language used on a [Kopaja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kopaja "Kopaja") bus advertisement

Indonesian is spoken as a [mother tongue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_language "First language") and national language. Over 200 million people regularly make use of the national language, with varying degrees of proficiency. In a nation that is home to more than 700 native languages and a vast array of ethnic groups, it plays an important unifying and cross-archipelagic role for the country. Use of the national language is abundant in the media, [government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government "Government") bodies, [schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School "School"), [universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University "University"), [workplaces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Workplace "Workplace"), among members of the upper-class or nobility and also in formal situations, despite the 2010 census showing only 19.94% of over-five-year-olds speak mainly Indonesian at home.[\[57\]](#ref-57)

Indonesian continues to function as the language of [national identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_identity "National identity") as the Congress of Indonesian Youth envisioned, and also serves as the language of education, [literacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literacy "Literacy"), [modernization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernization_theory "Modernization theory"), and [social mobility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_mobility "Social mobility").[\[48\]](#ref-Paauw) Standard Indonesian is used in books and newspapers and on television/radio news broadcasts. The standard dialect, however, is rarely used in daily conversations, being confined mostly to formal settings. While this is a phenomenon common to most languages in the world (for example, spoken English does not always correspond to its written standards), the proximity of spoken Indonesian (in terms of grammar and vocabulary) to its normative form is noticeably low, hence fits into the [Charles Ferguson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_A._Ferguson "Charles A. Ferguson")'s [diglossia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diglossia "Diglossia"), although there is a continuum between the standard Indonesian and colloquial Indonesian.[\[17\]](#ref-Sneddon-2003) This is mostly due to Indonesians combining aspects of their own local languages (e.g., [Javanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javanese_language "Javanese language"),[\[58\]](#ref-58) [Sundanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sundanese_language "Sundanese language"), and [Balinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balinese_language "Balinese language"))[\[59\]](#ref-59) with Indonesian.[\[17\]](#ref-Sneddon-2003) This results in various vernacular varieties of Indonesian, the very types that a foreigner is most likely to hear upon arriving in any Indonesian city or town.[\[17\]](#ref-Sneddon-2003)[\[60\]](#ref-60) Javanese influence will have more lasting effect on the future development of Indonesian as the coinage of new terms from a language of Indonesia is preferred, Javanese is immediately acceptable for around half of Indonesian, Javan \[educational\] infrastructure has always been better, and the number of influential Javanese officials in government and military.[\[44\]](#ref-:5) Javanese has impacted on the lexicon of Jakartanese specifically from _ngoko_ register of Javanese and which flows later into colloquial Indonesian, while nothing come from _krama_ register of Javanese.[\[61\]](#ref-:7) Meanwhile, [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit "Sanskrit")– [Old Javanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Javanese "Old Javanese") language (beside Western European languages) —instead of [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic "Arabic") such in [Malaysian Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Malay "Malaysian Malay")— become new major source of new words in standard Indonesia, such as _bhayangkara_ (police), _purnawirawan_ (military–police retiree), _dwiwarna_ (literally bicolor, refers to the national flag), which acts such as _krama inggil_ register of Javanese which serves an honorofic function and confers prestige on referents and linked to esoteric knowledge and social power of traditional Javanese conception of _krama inggil_ register of Javanese which usage of older prestigious codes to exhibit extraordinary esoteric knowledge and hence legitimizing elite with access into it,[\[61\]](#ref-:7)[\[62\]](#ref-:10)[\[63\]](#ref-:9) which was detested by some for feudality as it impedes construction of simple and efficient language and modern democratic idea.[\[63\]](#ref-:9) Another criticism for Indonesian development is abundant use of acronyms as nobody really knows all of them.[\[63\]](#ref-:9) This phenomenon is amplified by the use of [Indonesian slang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_slang "Indonesian slang"), particularly in the cities. Unlike the relatively uniform standard variety, Vernacular Indonesian exhibits a high degree of geographical variation, though Jakartan Colloquial Indonesian functions as the _de facto_ norm of informal language and is a major source of influence throughout the archipelago.[\[17\]](#ref-Sneddon-2003) Meanwhile, there is [language shift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Language_shift "Language shift") of [first language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_language "First language") among Indonesian into Indonesian from other languages in Indonesia caused by ethnic diversity and urbanicity.[\[64\]](#ref-64)

The most common and widely used colloquial Indonesian is heavily influenced by the [Betawi language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Betawi_language "Betawi language"), a [Malay-based creole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_trade_and_creole_languages "Malay trade and creole languages") of [Jakarta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jakarta "Jakarta"), amplified by its popularity in Indonesian popular culture in mass media and Jakarta's status as the national capital.[\[17\]](#ref-Sneddon-2003)[\[44\]](#ref-:5) In informal spoken Indonesian, various words are replaced with those of a less formal nature. For example, _tidak_ (no) is often replaced with the Betawi form _nggak_ or the even simpler _gak/ga_, while _seperti_ (like, similar to) is often replaced with _kayak_[\[kajaʔ\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Malay "Help:IPA/Malay"). _Sangat_ or _amat_ (very), the term to express intensity, is often replaced with the Javanese-influenced _banget_. As for pronunciation, the diphthongs _ai_ and _au_ on the end of base words are typically pronounced as /e/ and /o/. In informal writing, the spelling of words is modified to reflect the actual pronunciation in a way that can be produced with less effort. For example, _capai_ becomes _cape_ or _capek_, _pakai_ becomes _pake_, _kalau_ becomes _kalo_. In verbs, the prefix _me-_ is often dropped, although an initial nasal consonant is often retained, as when _mengangkat_ becomes _ngangkat_ (the base word is _angkat_). The suffixes _-kan_ and _-i_ are often replaced by _-in_.[\[17\]](#ref-Sneddon-2003) For example, _mencarikan_ becomes _nyariin_, _menuruti_ becomes _nurutin_. The latter grammatical aspect is one often closely related to the Indonesian spoken in Jakarta and its surrounding areas.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

| English | Standard Indonesian | Colloquial Indonesian |
| --- | --- | --- |
| no, not | tidak | nggak |
| big | besar | gede |
| very | sekali | banget |
| just | saja | aja |
| how | bagaimana | gimana |
| like | seperti | kayak |
| want | ingin | pengen |
| make | membuat | bikin |
| say | mengatakan | bilang |
| give | memberi | kasih |

Comparison of Standard Indonesian and Jakartan Colloquial Indonesian[\[17\]](#ref-Sneddon-2003)

Western European languages (such as Dutch and English) become new major source of new words in standard Indonesian, which allows Indonesian to refer things more precisely and economical.[\[61\]](#ref-:7) The usage of Dutch words in Malay became a symbol of high social status which remain until years after independence despite heavy critic against it.[\[44\]](#ref-:5) As American education has gained highest status value, Amran Halim warns against excessive and unnecessary use of English which cause superfluous borrowing such as _mengakselerasikan_ instead of _mempercepat_ (to accelerate) hence resulted in Salim's Indo-Saxonization as it connotes prestige and status.[\[44\]](#ref-:5)[\[61\]](#ref-:7) Despite such warning, there is Indonesian attitude to use foreign words while there are available Indonesian words because the usage of foreign words is seen as sign of social achievement, unlike English postcolonial linguistic xenophobia in [Malaysian Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Malay "Malaysian Malay") of 1950–60s, and more pronounced than [laissez-faire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laissez-faire "Laissez-faire") of [Thais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_language "Thai language").[\[44\]](#ref-:5)[\[61\]](#ref-:7) Heavy influence of Western European languages can be linked to esoteric knowledge and social power of traditional Javanese conception of _krama inggil_ register of Javanese which usage of older prestigious codes to exhibit extraordinary esoteric knowledge and hence legitimizing elite with access into it.[\[61\]](#ref-:7)[\[62\]](#ref-:10) Further encroachment of English words in Indonesian raises a form of code mixing between Indonesian and English known as _bahasa Jaksel_ ( _Jaksel_ means _Jakarta Selatan_, [South Jakarta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Jakarta "South Jakarta")),[\[65\]](#ref-65) which is also known as Indoglish.[\[66\]](#ref-66)

## Classification and related languages

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Indonesian_language&action=edit&section=8 "Edit section: Classification and related languages")\]

See also: [Austronesian languages §Cross-linguistic Comparison Chart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austronesian_languages#Cross-linguistic_Comparison_Chart "Austronesian languages")

Malay historical linguists agree on the likelihood of the Malay homeland being in western [Borneo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borneo "Borneo").[\[67\]](#ref-67) A form known as Proto-Malay language was spoken in Borneo at least by 1000 BCE and was, it has been argued, the ancestral language of all subsequent [Malayan languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malayic_languages "Malayic languages"). Its ancestor, [Proto-Malayo-Polynesian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Malayo-Polynesian_language "Proto-Malayo-Polynesian language"), a descendant of the [Proto-Austronesian language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Austronesian_language "Proto-Austronesian language"), began to break up by at least 2000 BCE, possibly as a result of the southward expansion of [Austronesian peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austronesian_peoples "Austronesian peoples") into [Maritime Southeast Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maritime_Southeast_Asia "Maritime Southeast Asia") from the island of [Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan "Taiwan").[\[68\]](#ref-68) Indonesian, which originated from Malay, is a member of the [Austronesian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austronesian_languages "Austronesian languages") family of languages, which includes languages from [Southeast Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Asia "Southeast Asia"), the [Pacific Ocean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Ocean "Pacific Ocean") and [Madagascar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madagascar "Madagascar"), with a smaller number in continental [Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asia "Asia"). The formal register has a high degree of [mutual intelligibility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mutual_intelligibility "Mutual intelligibility") with the Malaysian standard of Malay, which is officially known there as _[bahasa Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_Malay "Malaysian Malay")_, despite the numerous lexical differences.[\[69\]](#ref-69) However, vernacular varieties spoken in Indonesia and Malaysia share limited intelligibility, which is evidenced by the fact that Malaysians have difficulties understanding Indonesian [sinetron (soap opera)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soap_opera "Soap opera") aired on Malaysia TV stations, and vice versa.[\[70\]](#ref-70) This gap is further widened by a significant degree of [diglossia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diglossia "Diglossia"), as the use of informal registers and slang ( _Bahasa Gaul_) in media, such as soap operas and animated series, differs markedly from the formal standard.

[Malagasy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malagasy_language "Malagasy language"), a geographic outlier spoken in [Madagascar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madagascar "Madagascar") in the [Indian Ocean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Ocean "Indian Ocean"); the Philippines national language, [Filipino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_language "Filipino language"); [Formosan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formosan_languages "Formosan languages") in Taiwan's aboriginal population; and the native [Māori language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_language "Māori language") of New Zealand are also members of this language family. Although each language of the family is mutually unintelligible, their similarities are rather striking. Many roots have come virtually unchanged from their common ancestor, [Proto-Austronesian language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Austronesian_language "Proto-Austronesian language"). There are many [cognates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognate "Cognate") found in the languages' words for kinship, health, body parts and common animals. Numbers, especially, show remarkable similarities.
